  on Windows yet.
- **`--prefill-head-alias` (`--serve`, one GPU): the output head's VRAM holds the prompt buffers while a prompt is read**,
  and the head is read back in before the first generated token. It was not measured with the paired method above. On
  an 8 GB RTX PRO 2000 Laptop (Windows, IQ3_XXS, 590 MiB reserve) it let the prompt path use 2,048-token chunks
  instead of 512: prefill 292.5 -> 667.1 tok/s on a 29,854-token prompt (medians of 12 and 6 fresh-engine trials).
  Reads of up to 512 fresh tokens cost about 0.6-0.75 s more per request, and the card had 0 MiB free while decoding.
  Details, limits and the measurement: [PREFILL_HEAD_ALIAS.md](PREFILL_HEAD_ALIAS.md).
  `STRATA_ADAPT_NOWAIT` or `STRATA_DMA_BATCH`; otherwise the log says why it is off. RTX A4000, 6 interleaved pairs each,
  decode median tok/s: Q2_0 16 GB 62.5 -> 66.2 (+5.9%), Q2_0 as a 12 GB card 51.3 -> 54.2 (+5.7%), IQ3_XXS 16 GB 48.6 -> 52.6 (+8.3%),
  IQ3_XXS as 12 GB 39.3 -> 41.5 (+5.5%), 6/6 pairs faster every time.
- **`STRATA_MMQ_DIRECT=1`: the prompt's MMQ reads resident experts in their cache slots** (CUDA, native packs; needs an
  engine built with `-DSTRATA_MMQ_TAB=ON`, which is off by default). The prompt path multiplies its experts in groups of
  16 with llama.cpp's MMQ, which wants a group's weights in one buffer at a fixed stride, so every expert a chunk routes
  to is copied there first (2.3-3.1 MB each, per chunk and layer), resident ones too. With this on, a PolyStrata-side copy of
  MMQ's outer scheduler (`src/prefill/moe_mmq_tab.cuh`) takes one pointer per expert instead, and a resident expert is
  read where it is; streamed, peer-GPU and other experts are still copied. The launch (tile size, stream-k split,
  fixup) is the one MMQ makes for the copied group and the tile code is llama.cpp's own, so the answers are the same
  bits (`prefill_mmq_tab_test`: 874 products bitwise equal to the copied path, all nine types - Q2_0, IQ2_XXS, IQ2_XS,
  IQ2_S, IQ3_XXS, IQ3_S, IQ4_NL, IQ4_XS, Q8_0 - and every tile size; the optional Q5_0 and K-quant experts get no
  pointer table and keep the copy). Each sized cache slot keeps 256 bytes after its expert, because MMQ reads up to
  136 bytes past a 640-wide down matrix; they are zeroed before each group. That is about 2.3 MiB over 9,300 slots (one
  slot fewer out of 6,998 on one RTX 3090). `STRATA_MMQ_DIRECT=2` keeps that layout but copies as before (an A/B
  reference); 0 or unset is the default path, byte for byte. `STRATA_MMQ_DIRECT_LOG=1` adds each stage's count of experts
  read in place to the log after every prompt. Under HIP, on a build without `STRATA_MMQ_TAB`, or when CMake turned
  that option off (it does so, with a message, when the llama.cpp tree's `mmq.cuh` differs from the one the copy
  mirrors), the variable is ignored.
  **Why the build option is off:** the engine loads every kernel at start (`CUDA_MODULE_LOADING=EAGER`), and the nine
  pointer-table instances (+15 MB of binary) take 36-38 MiB of VRAM on every GPU (sm_86), with the variable unset too -
  expert slots every model would lose, as with `STRATA_MMQ_KQUANTS`. They also cost ~70% of the upstream MMQ instances'
  compile time per CUDA arch (175 s of CPU at sm_86). Without the option the device code is byte for byte that of a
  build without the feature.
  Measured on 4x RTX 3090, UD-IQ4_XS, split `12,24,36` with `--trim-stage-weights` and `--batch 16` (every expert
  resident), the engine driven directly, its "read in" time, medians of 8: a 170-token prompt 331 -> 251 ms (-24%) and
  369 -> 290 ms (-21%) in two series, a 1,217-token prompt 773 -> 634 ms (-18%) and 838 -> 675 ms (-19%). A hack that
  skipped the copies altogether ran 225 and 577 ms, but it is no bound for an exact implementation: it changes the
  routing (12,003 expert-layer entries fell to 777 on one prompt) and so does less work. On one RTX 3090 with most
  experts streamed from RAM the copies of streamed experts stay, and the prompt time did not move (within 1.5%).
  Exactness, with `STRATA_PREFILL_CPU_SHARE=0` and no adaptive swaps: the first decode window's logits after the prompt
  (`STRATA_DUMP_FIRST_LOGITS`, fp32) and 32 greedy tokens were byte-identical between 2 and 1 (and 0) for prompts of
  180-2,619 tokens - on the 4-GPU split with `--prefill 2048` (chunks of 1,024 tokens and more: the streamed walk),
  the same with 9 GB less expert cache per GPU (20-31% of the expert reads streamed, so groups mix in-place, copied and
  mid-group flushed experts), and on one GPU with `--expert-cache-per-layer`; the 150 greedy tokens of each of
  `tools/batch_test.py --n 16` prompts (solo and batch) were identical too, and to those of a build without the
  option. The uniform cache (no profile) holds no resident expert when a native pack's prompt is read (serve needs the
  profile, and a one-shot run fills the cache only while it decodes), so `expert_cache_slot_guard_test` covers it.

## Thread placement: `--aux-cpus` (Linux, off by default)

The CPU pool pins its workers (one logical CPU per physical core) and the host thread pins itself to the pool's reserved
core. A thread the engine starts after that inherits the host's one CPU: the adaptive tier's job thread, the prefill
helpers, the router look-ahead. CUDA driver threads created before pinning can run on any allowed CPU, the workers'
included; a lazily created host-callback thread can instead inherit the pinned host's one CPU (see the warm-up above).
`--aux-cpus` puts every thread that is neither a pool worker nor the host thread on spare CPUs. It moves threads only and
never changes a result.

- **Which CPUs.** `--aux-cpus auto` (or `STRATA_AUX_CPUS=auto`): the SMT siblings of the host's core first, then the CPUs of
  physical cores that have no pool worker. A CPU that shares a core with a worker is never taken; when no CPU is spare
  the feature does nothing and the start-up line says so. A list (`--aux-cpus 24,26` or `24-27`) is used as given, minus
  the host's CPU and the workers' CPUs, and only CPUs the process may run on. `--aux-cpus off` is the default.
- **How.** Threads the engine starts pin themselves first thing: the adaptive tier's job thread and its per-round
  threads, the prefill copy threads and helpers, the router look-ahead, the PLE reader, the file readers, the stdin reader
  and the watchdog. The threads someone else started (the CUDA driver's) are moved by a sweep over `/proc/self/task`
  once the pool exists and again at the start of every request. The pool's workers and the host thread are never moved,
  and neither is a thread somebody pinned to one CPU. An explicit `STRATA_ADAPT_JOB_CPU` still wins for the adaptive tier's
  job thread. `STRATA_AUX_STAGER=0` leaves the prefill copy threads where they were started: with a one-CPU spare set up
  to 32 of them (`STRATA_STAGER_THREADS`) would share that CPU, which can slow the read of a long prompt.
- **Per request.** `"strata_tune": {"aux_cpus": 1}` (`serve/server.py` forwards `1`, `0`, `true` and `false`) moves the
  threads to the set; `0` puts every moved thread back to the placement it had. A request without the key goes back to the
  start-up setting. With `--batch` the most recently admitted request's value holds, as for the other `strata_tune` keys.
  An engine started without `--aux-cpus` plans the set anyway, so `1` takes `auto`.
- **What it prints.** At start: `strata aux cpus: threads that are neither pool workers nor the host go to CPUs 24 (on;
  N threads placed so far)`, or `(off at start; a request's aux_cpus=1 turns it on)`. A request that changes the state
  prints `strata serve: aux cpus ON/off for this request`.
- **Not covered.** Other processes (the Python launcher, a container runtime) and the engine's host thread.
- **Measured.** One machine: 2x RTX 3080 20 GB at 220 W, Xeon E5-2696 v4 (24 logical CPUs in the container, 16 pool
  workers each on its own CPU), UD-Q4_K_XL, layer split 23, `--batch-mtp`, two slots, adaptive tier on. `auto` put the
  host thread on CPU 2 and the helper threads on CPU 24. Same binary in every arm, only the flag differs. Two pairs of
  whole engine restarts in the order `auto`, off, `auto`, off (12 solo decodes and 10 two-stream rounds per restart,
  medians): solo decode 79.7 and 80.4 tok/s with `auto` against 72.1 and 72.4 off (off / `auto` = 0.904 and 0.900), two
  streams in aggregate 90.8 and 90.7 against 80.8 and 82.8 (0.890 and 0.912). The same configuration restarted twice
  moved solo by 1.009 and 1.005 and two streams by 0.999 and 1.024. Live on one engine, switching per request with
  `strata_tune` and rotating the order (20 solo requests per arm, 16 two-stream rounds): off / on = 0.888 solo and
  0.917 two streams, against 0.985 and 0.990 for an A/A control of the same setting. The host thread's involuntary
  context switches over a bench of about ten minutes were 43.8k and 42.8k with the flag off and 3.9k and 2.8k with
  `auto`. Prompt reads (24K, 25K, 51K, 104K) do not depend on the flag: off is +0.7% to +1.5% against `auto`, inside the
  restart noise. The mean and the p99 of the doorbell-to-flag time are not worse without the flag; what appears is rare
  stalls above 100 us, in 7 and 8 of 12 requests off against 0 or 1 with `auto`. That preemption of the host causes the
  loss is an inference; the counters do not prove it. The warm-up requests of each run were discarded.

---

## Experimental speed projection (EXPERIMENTAL, off by default)

**This is an experiment, not a finished feature.** It ships with PolyStrata but stays off unless you turn it on.

A 480 KB control vector for Qwen3.8-Flash-Next (`data/experimental-speed-projection/`, see its README). After each
of layers 4-44 the engine removes one direction from every hyper-connection stream of the residual: `h -= (h . v) v`,
one unit vector `v` per layer, exactly as llama.cpp does with the package's `--cvec-mode project` patches.

**What it changes.** The vector's own package describes it as a **refusal-direction projection**: with it the model
declines far fewer requests (it reports 1 of 50 vs 50 of 50 on its test set), and removing refusals removes a safety
behaviour - you are responsible for what the model writes with it on. It also shifts ordinary answers a little
(measured below). It is not an optimization in the engine: on the same text it costs 0.2-0.4% per token. What a
chat's tokens/s does with it on depends on the text the model writes (length, repetition, how well the drafts land),
so measure it on your own prompts; the Monitor marks every request ESP or stock.

**Turning it on (at setup).** `START-HERE.bat --setup` asks "Turn on the experimental speed projection?" (default:
no), or pass `--experimental-speed-projection on` (`off`, or a path to another vector GGUF). Only for the original
Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, not Swift 1.5. It writes these engine flags (llama.cpp's) into `strata-<model>.json`:

```
--control-vector-scaled <PolyStrata>\data\experimental-speed-projection\Qwen3.8-Flash-Next-experimental-speed-projection.gguf:1.0
--control-vector-layer-range 4 44 --cvec-mode project --cvec-dir per-layer
```

The engine log then says `control vector mode = project, dir = per-layer, layers 4..44 (41 steered)`, and the web
app's About tab lists it. (`--cvec-mode add` is llama.cpp's stock additive mode, for additive vectors.)

**Per request.** A loaded vector is on for every request unless it says otherwise: the web app's Sampling drawer has
a switch, and the API takes `"experimental_speed_projection": false` in the request body (OpenAI and Anthropic; a
config default goes in `"sampling": {"experimental_speed_projection": false}`). Switching drops the conversation
cache once, since the model state was computed the other way. Switched off, the output is token-for-token the stock
model's.

**Measured here** (Q2_0, fixed experts, 2,557 teacher-forced tokens of code, a document and a chat): the top-1 token
changes at 10% of positions, mean KL from the stock model 0.063 nats (max 4.1), perplexity +15% on code, +2.3% on
the document, +0.4% on the chat. Details: `bench/results/2026-09-27-esp/`.

---

## Troubleshooting

| Symptom | What to do |
| --- | --- |
| `the NVIDIA driver is too old` | Update the driver (NVIDIA App or nvidia.com/drivers), restart, run `START-HERE.bat` again. |
| Python or the build tools could not be installed | Install what it names (links are printed), then run it again. Everything already done is kept. |
| `port 8080 is already in use` | PolyStrata is already running (look for its window), or another program uses the port: `START-HERE.bat --port 8081`. |
| `cudaHostRegister ... out of memory` in the log | Normal on Windows: the engine pins the experts in per-layer slices instead. Only a problem if the load then fails. |
| `ExpertCache: cudaMalloc(...) failed: out of memory` although VRAM is free | Windows' page file is off or tiny: every allocation on the graphics card is also charged to Windows' commit (RAM + page file). Set a fixed page file size, the same initial and maximum size (e.g. 65536 MB: System > About > Advanced system settings > Performance > Advanced > Virtual memory > Custom size), and restart: a page file Windows grows on demand ("System managed", or an initial size below the maximum) may not grow in time while the card's memory is charged (#60: "System managed" and 4096-32768 MB still failed, a fixed 64 GB worked). Since 0.1.19 the engine retries with a smaller cache instead of stopping, and setup warns about a page file under 4 GB (issue #60). |
| The first start takes minutes | It is reading 34-55 GB into RAM; the second start is faster while the files are in the OS cache. |
| The PC freezes for a few minutes at the start | Normal, most of all the first time (the server window says when it happens): the engine loads the experts into RAM, pins part of it for the GPU and sizes the expert cache. Wait; don't close the window. Still frozen after 10 minutes: restart the PC, close other programs, try again, or pick a smaller size. |
| `the engine stopped unexpectedly (exit code ...)` | The engine process ended mid-answer - usually out of RAM (Linux ends the biggest program: `sudo dmesg \| grep -i -E 'killed process\|out of memory'`). The next request starts it again by itself. If it repeats: close other programs or pick a smaller size. The server also warns at start when the model's experts leave less than ~6 GB of RAM for everything else. |
| Slow output, disk light busy | Not enough free RAM: close other programs, or choose Q2_0 / IQ2_XS. |
| `prompt ... exceeds the context` | The request is longer than the context you chose: run setup again with a bigger `--context`. |
| `the setup refuses --rope-scaling none for a past-trained context` | A context past the trained 262,144 needs the rotary angles rescaled (experimental rope scaling), and the setup will not configure one with the stock angles there. Let it pick (`START-HERE.bat --setup --context 393216` adds yarn and a covering factor), or pass `--rope-scaling linear` or `yarn` yourself. |
| Slower than the tables | The monitor plugged into the GPU and other GPU programs take VRAM from the expert cache; RAM running below its rated speed (enable EXPO/XMP in the BIOS) slows the CPU half. |
| `this server was started without the vision encoder` | The model was set up for text only: run setup again with `--vision gpu`. |
| Setup puts the model on an old drive after you moved or reinstalled PolyStrata (#1070) | Setup remembers the data folder you used last in its settings file (`%APPDATA%\PolyStrata\settings.json` on Windows, `~/.config/strata/settings.json` on Linux; the `data_dir` entry) and uses it again. Choose another place with `--data-dir DIR` (model files, packs, MTP layer) or `--models-dir DIR` (the GGUF files only), or delete the `data_dir` line; `--gguf-dir DIR` uses GGUF files you already have without copying. To move a model, copy its folder and run setup with the new `--data-dir`. |
| A picture is refused or `cannot read the image` | The file is not a picture Pillow can open (JPEG, PNG, WebP, GIF, BMP, TIFF, AVIF work). |
| Pictures are slow (3-30 s) | The encoder runs on the CPU: run setup again with `--vision gpu` (needs ~1.4 GB of VRAM). |
| A request never finishes: "reading the prompt", GPU "100%" at low power | The GPU ran out of VRAM (engines before 0.1.9 could end with ~30 MiB free at large contexts). Run `START-HERE.bat` once to get engine 0.1.9 or newer; the log then says `... MiB of VRAM free with everything loaded` (a few hundred) and names the `--vram-reserve-mib` to add if it is low. |
| Output much slower than the tables above, only with a large `--context`, and the start says a small `... MiB of VRAM free with everything loaded` | The expert cache does not fit the card at that context. With `--expert-cache auto` the engine shrinks it to fit; with a fixed number it does not check again after the slots are written, and on Windows the extra is put in system memory instead of failing, so only the speed shows it. Use `auto`, a smaller number, or raise `--vram-reserve-mib` (it is deducted before the cache is sized). See "The expert cache size is a budget" under Sharing the GPU. |
| Generation stops mid-answer, GPU "100%", one CPU core busy | Fixed in engine 0.1.12 (issue #29, a race in the CPU expert pool on big-VRAM cards). Since then a request that stops moving ends with an error instead of hanging (after 2 minutes; 1 minute from 0.1.13): the log says `no progress for ... s ... (issue #29)` with where it stopped, and the next request starts the engine again. If you see that line, please open an issue with it. Engine 0.1.13 adds a stall report under it (what every expert-pool thread and the GPU handshake were doing, memory and page faults) and, on Windows, a `strata-stall-<pid>.dmp` file with every thread's stack: attach both. (`STRATA_WATCHDOG_S` sets the time in seconds; 0 turns it off.) Engine 0.1.14 fixes the stall those reports found (issue #31: with the IQ packs the host could wait forever inside the NVIDIA driver while copying experts in a verify window; the experts are now copied by a GPU kernel, `--pcie-mode dma` restores the old way). |
| `no progress for 60 s ... reading the prompt` on Linux, and the stall report says `threads waiting on the disk (state D): 16 ...` | The engine waits for the drive, not a deadlock: the n-gram table is read at random (`--ple-io direct`), which a rotational disk cannot keep up with (#605). The engine warns at start when the table is on one; `--ple-io ram` (Linux, needs RAM for the table) or the model on an SSD fixes it. Setup adds `--ple-io ram` itself on a rotational disk when the RAM holds the table (0.1.39). |
| `the engine said nothing for ... s during the request` or `... did not finish the request after it was stopped (STOP)` | Issue #481: the engine and the server lost step (the engine waits for its next command, the server for the request's end; GPU at 0 %, nothing in the log). The server ends the engine after 300 s without a line from it during a request (while a prompt is read: each chunk may take three times the previous one's time, the first one up to its tokens at 50 tok/s more), the request ends with an error and the next request starts the engine again. `"engine_silence_s": 600` in `strata-<model>.json` sets the time (0 = wait forever, as before). If you see it, please add the end of the engine log to #481. |
| `the engine said nothing for ... s and used no CPU or disk in that time (frozen ...)` | Issue #1317: a quicker version of the check above for an engine that is not slow but stopped (a deadlock inside a CUDA call, a driver stall): after 90 s without a line, if the engine process has also used no CPU time and moved no disk bytes in that time, the server ends it and the next request starts it again. An engine that is silent but still working is never ended by this check, the server prints one note (`... but is still working; it is not ended`) and leaves it to `engine_silence_s`. `STRATA_ENGINE_STALL_S=180` sets the time, `0` turns the check off (it needs `psutil`, which setup installs). |
| `out of memory: cudaFuncSetAttribute` in the log (IQ3_XXS, long prompt) | Fixed in engine 0.1.15: CUDA loaded a kernel's code when it was first needed, and mid-prompt there was no VRAM left for it. Run `START-HERE.bat` (Windows) or `./setup.sh` (Linux) once to update. |
| Anything else | The engine log is `strata-<model>.log` in this folder. |

---

## How it works

<p align="center"><img src="paper/tiers.svg" width="760" alt="memory tiers"></p>

- **GPU (VRAM):** attention and DeltaNet mixers, the gated-residual weights, routers, shared experts, output head, the MTP
  draft layer, the KV cache (from 64K: only its most-read part, the rest streams from RAM), and an **expert cache** that fills the rest of VRAM with the most-used experts (it adapts to
  the conversation while you chat).
- **RAM:** all 24,576 experts, pinned. The CPU computes the experts that are not on the GPU **in place**, at the same time
  as the GPU works on the cached ones (AVX-512 / AVX2 kernels, ggml's for the i-quants).
- **SSD:** the 28.8 GB n-gram table, a few rows per token, read unbuffered past the OS cache (`--ple-io direct`, the
  default, made for SSDs; on a rotational disk `--ple-io ram` keeps the table in RAM, #605). A start-up probe warns when direct reads measure slow (under 15K rows/s) but never switches by itself (since 0.1.43.1; `STRATA_PLE_AUTO_MMAP=1` brings back the 0.1.42 switch to `--ple-io mmap`): the bar is calibrated for NVMe, a SATA SSD tops out at 10-14K rows/s with direct's 16 reads in flight, and on one (Linux, 77.8K-token prompt) mmap read 2.1K rows/s against direct's 14.4K (#2040), while on a Windows box with a slow mapped file mmap stalled prompts (#1984). The engine reads the table
  in the format the GGUF has it: IQ4_NL (the default table), Q4_0, Q5_0, Q5_1, Q8_0, FP8 (E4M3 with a scale) or BF16.
  Measured on 4,000 random rows against the checkpoint's own BF16 table, the mean per-row error is Q8_0 0.53%, FP8 2.64%,
  Q5_1 3.78%, Q5_0 4.25%, IQ4_NL 7.60%, Q4_1 7.80%, Q4_0 8.55% (a Q8_0 table is 54 GB, an IQ4_NL one 28.8 GB). On the Q2_0 model, 700 teacher-forced tokens, the mean KL against the BF16 table is 0.0117 (Q5_0), 0.0121 (Q5_1), 0.0125
  (Q8_0), 0.0128 (FP8), 0.0140 (Q4_0) and 0.0146 (IQ4_NL), with the perplexity within 1% of BF16's either way: any change to the
  table moves the 2-bit model by about 0.012, so the formats are hard to tell apart. Setup does not offer another table;
  it only changes which GGUF the engine is given.
- **Speculation:** the model's own MTP layer drafts up to 3 tokens; one pass over all 48 layers checks them. 2.4-3.2
  tokens per pass on average. When the reply repeats the context (code edits, quoted text), **prompt lookup** (engine
  0.1.7) drafts up to 5 tokens from the earlier copy, but only where its measured acceptance and cost say it pays:
  code edits 6-11% faster, other text unchanged. The drafts are checked like the MTP's, so the output is the same.
- **Prompts** are processed in chunks of up to 8,192 tokens (`--prefill auto`; 32,768 opt-in) with the experts
  streamed to the GPU over PCIe. Since engine 0.1.39b (#583) the streamed ring is sized in bytes for the pack (a
  native pack with bigger blobs gets fewer ring slots and keeps more cache slots) and `--prefill auto` picks the
  largest chunk that keeps that ring full. It only gives up ring slots where 0.1.39's rule held the chunk at 4,096
  tokens or less; from 6,144 on it keeps 0.1.39's ring and only looks for a bigger chunk beside it (shrinking the
  ring there measured slower: the Coder -12%), and a prompt that fits 0.1.39's chunk keeps 0.1.39's ring (one
  chunk: a smaller ring only slowed it). RTX 5070, 32K prompts against `STRATA_RING_BYTES=0`, 2-3 interleaved
  rounds: IQ3_XXS +18.5% at a 1,500-slot cache and +9% in another memory state, the Coder +3%, IQ3_S unchanged; 4K
  prompts unchanged (-0.7% to +1.1%, bit-identical). Q2_0 (a fixed `--prefill 2048`) is not affected. It changes
  the bits of a long prompt against 0.1.39 (the experts go through a different
  mix of cached and streamed groups). Measured quality, teacher-forced over the next 2,001 tokens of a long document
  against the FP16 prompt path (IQ3_XXS, `--prefill auto`): 8K prompt KL 0.042 (0.1.39: 0.054), top-1 agreement
  93.5% (92.2%); 32K prompt KL 0.020 (0.019), top-1 95.3% (95.0%) - the same band as before. `STRATA_RING_BYTES=0`
  restores 0.1.39's ring, loan and chunk choice.

The full story, with measurements, bottlenecks and what comes next: **[docs/paper/Strata-Paper.pdf](paper/Strata-Paper.pdf)**.

### Start the API without loading the model

`serve/server.py --engine strata --config strata-<model>.json --lazy` (or `"lazy_load": true` in that
config) starts the lightweight HTTP API without starting the native engine or the vision encoder. The first
generation request, or `POST /load`, starts the vision encoder first when configured, then loads the engine through
the existing reload path, including `before_load` and `min_free_vram_mib`. This also works with image requests:
the load finishes before PolyStrata encodes the image. `POST /unload` stops both processes. Eager startup remains the
default.

`POST /v1/load` and `/v1/unload` are JSON control aliases for integrations, accepting `{}` or
`{"model":"<configured model>"}` and returning model status. They require the configured API key,
`application/json`, and no foreign browser Origin. They return **409** while a request is active or queued,
and **404** for an unknown model. Existing `/load` and `/unload` behavior is preserved. `/api/health` aliases
`/health`; `/v1/status` exposes `loaded` and `auto_load`. The unloaded model remains discoverable.

Unloading and shutdown close the native engine's stdin after sending `QUIT`, allowing Windows' detached
stdin reader to see EOF. Cleanup waits for process exit before releasing handles; if forced shutdown still
times out, the server keeps ownership and reports an error rather than claiming the model was unloaded.

### JSON response formats

`POST /v1/chat/completions` accepts `response_format: {"type":"json_object"}` or
`{"type":"json_schema","json_schema":{"name":"answer","strict":true,"schema":{"type":"object","properties":{"answer":{"type":"integer"}},"required":["answer"],"additionalProperties":false}}}`.
The schema must accept only JSON objects at its root: `"type":"object"`, or an `anyOf`/`oneOf` whose branches are all
object schemas (an `allOf` with an object member, or a local `$ref` to one, also counts), as apps written for
llama.cpp's `json_schema` send. A root that also allows an array, string, number, boolean or null is refused. Local
`#` references work; remote references are refused.
`json_schema` is checked with the Python package `jsonschema` when it is installed (`python -m pip install
"jsonschema>=4.23,<5"`; setup does not add it); without it the answer is only checked to be one JSON object, and the
server says so once.

This is **schema prompting followed by server validation**, not grammar-constrained decoding. One generation
is made per request, with no hidden retry. Successful responses contain a validated JSON object. Malformed JSON,
duplicate keys, non-finite numbers, schema violations and incomplete generations return **502** with
`error.code: structured_output_failed`; invalid request schemas return **400**. JSON formats combined with
tools/MCP are refused explicitly. Without `response_format`, ordinary text and tool behavior stays the same.

Structured SSE buffers the answer while sending keep-alive comments. It emits content only after validation,
then usage/timings and `[DONE]`; failures emit an SSE error and `[DONE]` without invalid content deltas.
`/v1/status.structured_output` advertises the formats, validation method and buffered streaming behavior.

### API request monitor

Off by default, since it keeps prompts and answers in memory: turn it on with `"api_monitor": true` in
`strata-<model>.json` (or `serve/server.py --api-monitor`); otherwise nothing is recorded and the two endpoints below
answer 404.
Open `/api-monitor` to inspect API traffic without opening a chat. It shows the model state, safe
load/unload controls, active/queued requests, original request bodies, output, separate reasoning and
non-stream response bodies. Total wall-clock includes FIFO waits and automatic loading; load, queue,
first-token, prompt/output tokens and engine decode timing are shown separately.

`GET /api/requests` returns compact summaries; `GET /api/requests?id=<id>` returns one retained request.
Both use the existing API-key check. The monitor retains the newest **100 requests in memory** until restart,
with **262,144 characters per input/output/reasoning/response field** and visible truncation flags. The actual API
responses are unaffected. Headers are not recorded, and the monitor key is kept in this tab's session storage.
Treat request history as sensitive input/output when exposing PolyStrata on a network: set an API key as above.
The page uses relative URLs and works through the existing host binding or a reverse proxy.

### Prompt buffers: `bo` shares `emb` (#1454)

The prompt path's half-output buffer `bo` reuses the embedding buffer `emb`, which is dead after the first hyper-connection broadcast: T x 2560 floats less VRAM per chunk (320 MiB at 32768 rows). The planner still counts those bytes by default, so the auto chunk and the cache slots the prompt path borrows are exactly those of 0.1.40.3 and the output bits are unchanged. `STRATA_EMB_REUSE_ACCOUNT=1` lets the planner use the saved bytes: where VRAM limits the chunk it grows (RTX 3060, IQ3_XXS: 6400 to 6656 tokens, 1652 to 1670 borrowed slots, prompt about +3.9%). A different chunk changes the prompt path's rounding, so prompt residuals are not byte-identical to the default; greedy output matched in our runs. Opt-in until it has a KL measurement.

**Fused int8 prompt experts on gfx12 (RDNA4, R9700): on by default for IQ3_S and IQ3_XXS packs.** `STRATA_PF_FUSED=0` turns them off (back to the MMQ prompt kernels, the previous bits). R9700, 1 card: prompt 4K +15.7%, 16K +13..+17%, 32K +19..+20%, a 96K prompt +16% (answers identical text); first-token KL against the FP16 prompt path over 96 prompts is equal to MMQ within noise (IQ3_XXS 0.00613 against 0.00683, IQ3_S 0.00416 against 0.00372). Other quants and other GPUs are unchanged; `STRATA_PF_FUSED=1` still opts in every covered format.

**gfx12 (Linux): a bare `--prefill auto` may take 16384-token chunks.** Explicit `--prefill N`, `auto:N` and `STRATA_PREFILL_AUTO_MAX` are untouched; `STRATA_GFX12_CHUNK=0` or `STRATA_GFX12_DEFAULTS=0` keeps 8192.

### CUDA diagnostics: a host thread for the verify-window launch

`STRATA_VERIFY_ASYNC_LAUNCH=1` runs `Verifier::run`'s CUDA graph launch on a separate
host thread. This lets its caller serve the CPU expert doorbells while an
instrumented `cudaGraphLaunch` blocks waiting for the graph. The caller joins the
launch and checks its real error before returning a frontier; an early service
failure releases the mapped GPU waits before joining. The switch is off by
default and applies to this single-request verifier entry point. Direct batch
and pipelined graph launches and the HIP/SYCL paths retain their existing scheduling.

For Compute Sanitizer, set this variable before running the usual model command
under `compute-sanitizer --tool memcheck --error-exitcode 86`. Build with
`STRATA_BUILD_TESTS=ON` and run `host_graph_launch_test` for the CUDA graph/CPU
signal, early cleanup and `--errors` error-propagation checks. Its `--synchronous`
mode is the negative control under memcheck: an eight-second watchdog releases
the original launch/service deadlock and the test fails.

## CUDA opt-in switches measured for 0.1.45 (RTX 3060 and RTX A4000, sm_86; all off unless you set them)

Ported from open pull requests and A/B'd as whole engine runs against the default (a fresh engine per side, IQ3_XXS, 5 pairs, medians;
bit-exact means the 10-prompt + 4K/20K identity gate with the switch on equals the gate with it off). Decode after a 30K-token prompt
unless noted; the gain is small on these two cards and larger on the Turing cards the authors measured.

| Switch | What | Answers | RTX 3060 / A4000 |
|---|---|---|---|
| `STRATA_ATTN_DAILY=1` (#2075) | decode attention: two cells per step, a merge that skips empty chunks | bit-exact | +3.2% (5/5) / +0.7% |
| `STRATA_QSA_ROWS=1` (#2059) | block scores of a verify window, queries in registers ([QSAROWS.md](QSAROWS.md)) | bit-exact | -0.3% / +0.3% |
| `STRATA_EXPDEC=1` (#2058) | gate/up of the window's resident experts with the q8_1 rows in shared memory ([EXPERTDEC.md](EXPERTDEC.md)) | bit-exact | +0.5% / +1 to +5% (short context) |
| `STRATA_SIDE_MIXER=1/2` (#1889) | the mixer's input-only work on graph branches | bit-exact | +0.5% / -0.6% (short context) |
| `STRATA_B6_MMVQ_ROWS=1` (#1418) | exact multi-column MMVQ reusing the activation fragment across rows (K-quants, IQ4_XS) | bit-exact | +0.2..1% / +0.5..2% |
| `STRATA_QSA_SPLIT_ATTN=1` (#1888) | decode attention split across blocks on the int8 mma path (sm_80+, int8 KV) | rounding-level (teacher-forced KL 0.0016 against a floor of 0.0012) | +2.8% / +3.5% |
| `STRATA_HC_UPMIX=1` (#1370) | the hyper-connection up projection and `gr_mix_r` in one kernel (sm_80+) | bit-exact here (gate identical) | prompt +0.6% / +0.4% |
| `STRATA_PROMPT_ATTN_IMMA=1` (#1367) | int8-KV prompt attention on INT8 tensor cores | rounding-level (KL at the floor) | prompt read +2.5% |
| `STRATA_HC_REQ8=1` (#1262) | the hyper-connection projections as int8 + a scale per 32 (more expert-cache slots) | rounding-level (KL 0.0018) | decode -0.2% / +2.2% |
| `STRATA_QUANT_GATHER=1` (#1368) | turns the once-per-token MoE input quantization back to the per-row gather | n/a | no difference measured |
| `STRATA_PREFILL_GROUP_GATHER=0` (#1107) | turns the short-prompt grouped gather off | n/a | on is 0.6-1.3% faster |

`STRATA_DEQUANT_WIDE` (the wide dequantization of Q4_K / Q5_K / Q6_K / IQ4_XS for the prompt's dense weights) is now on by default on
sm_86 as well as sm_61: bit-exact, and the time to the first token of a 150 / 300 / 450-token prompt fell 11-15% on the RTX 3060 and 6-10% on the RTX A4000 (5 of 5 pairs each).
`STRATA_DEQUANT_WIDE=0` turns it off.

### RTX 20 (Turing, sm_75) kernel series (opt-in, 0.1.45)

Kernels by rafatxf (#2069-#2072, #2093, #2220-#2222), imanu86 (#2081) and xrip (#1821) for compute capability 7.5. They were measured
on one RTX 2080 Ti (decode +5..6 % at 4K-32K, prompts at depth up to +30 %, per the PRs) and are bitwise equal to the kernels they
replace, but nobody on the team has an sm_75 card to confirm them, so they are off until asked for:

`STRATA_SM75_PERF=1` turns on every bitwise Turing kernel below on a 7.5 card (no other card is ever affected). Each has its own
variable, which wins in both directions (`=0` off, `=1` on): `STRATA_SM75_IL` (interleaved small-batch matvec table),
`STRATA_SM75_EXP` (expert layout mode 4), `STRATA_GR_UP75` / `STRATA_GR_DOWN_TG` (hyper-connection read), `STRATA_SCORES_HALVE`
and `STRATA_TOPK_SPLIT` (decode QSA selection), `STRATA_TOPK_SAMPLE` (prompt top-k, one read of the scores), `STRATA_ATTN_V3`
(decode attention), `STRATA_Q2_WARP` (Q2_0 matvec), `STRATA_FUSE_WAIT=2` and `STRATA_QIL` (fewer launches per decode layer),
`STRATA_TC_SM75` (BF16 dequant, 16-byte stores), `STRATA_PA_V1_PREFETCH` (prompt attention v1 prefetch),
`STRATA_SCORES_THREAD` (#1825: the prompt path's thread-per-head block scorer, bitwise the warp kernel's scores; `=1` takes it on any
CUDA card, where it measured +1..3 % prompt speed on a Tesla P100 at 30-60K tokens, so it is not a default there).

Options that change answers (other summation order) are separate and explicit: `STRATA_SELECT_SIMT=1|2` (FP32 tiled block scorer,
KL 0.01 against the warp scorer per #2071), `STRATA_PA_V2T=1` (v2 prompt attention, #2092/#2146), `STRATA_PF_FUSED=1
STRATA_PF_FUSED_SM75=1` (fused int8 prompt experts for the Q2_0 pack, #2145; only on a 7.5 card, sm_80+ is unchanged), `STRATA_BF16_TC=1` with `STRATA_XN_F16` (#2091).


# GLM 5.3 Flash (`strata-poly`)

PolyStrata adds a second engine, `strata-poly`, that runs GLM 5.3 Flash. It sits beside `strata`, which still runs
Qwen3.8-Flash-Next as before. The server, the web app and the API are the same for both: a run config names the
engine it starts.

It is early. This project has built and run it on one machine, with Linux and one NVIDIA card, and on two RTX
3080s; a user runs it on Windows with a 24 GB card and 128 GB of RAM ([issue 10](https://github.com/VecSzn/PolyStrata/issues/10)).

## What it was measured on

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Graphics card | RTX 5090, 32 GB |
| CPU | Xeon Platinum 8470Q, 25 vCPU of it (a cloud machine), 24 threads used |
| RAM | 120 GiB |
| Built with | CUDA 12.8 |
| Model | `GLM-5.3-Flash-GSQ-RCO-3.0bit-q4kattn.gguf`, 113.6 GB, from [neuralll/GLM-5.3-Flash-GSQ-RCO-3.0bit-Q4Kattn-GGUF](https://huggingface.co/neuralll/GLM-5.3-Flash-GSQ-RCO-3.0bit-Q4Kattn-GGUF) |
| Context | 32K |

| | `strata-poly` | llama.cpp ([neurall's fork](https://github.com/neurall/llama.cpp)), stock settings |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| Writes answers | 46 tokens/s after the prompt of 11,673 tokens and 62 to 66 after the one of 29,069, 62 to 65 for a 400-token answer to a short one | 26 tokens/s |
| Reads a prompt of 3,059 tokens | 641 to 648 tokens/s | about 265 tokens/s |
| Reads a prompt of 11,673 tokens | 760 to 817 tokens/s | |
| Reads a prompt of 29,069 tokens | 740 to 782 tokens/s | |
| Starts | about 7 s with the file in the page cache | |

Both ran on the machine above. Nothing here has been run on a smaller card.

The engine's numbers are two runs of the engine of 2ba29a383baf, with what setup gives it: the draft block, and
the expert profile the repository ships after one run has added its counts. A cell has the higher of these and
two runs of the build of 2026-10-06, which read the three prompts at 610 to 620, 788 to 802 and 733 to 742 tokens/s
and wrote 47 to 51 after the long ones; the 400-token answer is that build's, and came at 64 to 65 from this one.
The 29,069-token prompt starts like the 11,673-token one before it, so its first 10,240 tokens are not read
again. An earlier build, measured the same way without the draft block, wrote 26.7 to 27.7 tokens/s and read the
three prompts at 373, 334 and 312; [BACKENDS.md](BACKENDS.md#what-was-tuned) has what changed since. llama.cpp was
not run again.

The README's row for 3.0BIT is PolyStrata's own benchmark through the server, the test every model of this engine
gets ([BACKENDS.md](BACKENDS.md#a-prompt-of-30000-tokens)), with the engine of the ready-made release: a short chat
written at 59 to 66 tokens/s, and a prompt of 29,154 tokens read whole at 820 to 824, with the first token after
36 s and the answer after it written at 62 to 64. The build before read a prompt of 28,354 tokens there at 784 to
785, and the engine of 2ba29a383baf one of 29,364 at 786 to 810, with the first token after 36.3 to 37.5 s, and
wrote the short chat at 60 to 64 and the answer after the prompt at 61 to 62. Of the 29,069-token prompt in the
table above only the last 18,829 tokens are read, the ones furthest into the context, which is the likely reason
it comes out slower.

With greedy sampling the two write the same tokens until two candidates are nearly tied. The model's activations
are quantized to 8 bits between layers, so a difference in rounding between two correct implementations can decide
such a tie.

## What you need

- Linux and an NVIDIA card, RTX 20 or newer. It has only run on 32 GB of VRAM. About 12 GB go to the weights, the
  caches and the buffers before any expert, so setup stops and asks below 14 GB.
- RAM for the experts the card does not hold. The file is mapped and they are read from it while the model answers,
  so they should stay in the OS file cache: about 86 GB of the 107 GB on a 32 GB card. Setup's estimate for that card
  is 96 GB of RAM. It has run with 120 GB only. In a container, the memory limit must leave about 8 GB above the
  model's files: with less, answers read experts from the disk, and the container may end the engine
  ([MULTI_GPU.md](MULTI_GPU.md#the-strata-poly-models) has the pod it happened on). The engine says so at load.
- 114 GB of disk for the model file, and 3 GB for its draft block.

## Sizes

| Size | What it is | Download | RAM beside a 32 GB card |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: |
| `3.0BIT` | The whole model, 288 routed experts a layer: the 3-bit file from [neuralll](https://huggingface.co/neuralll/GLM-5.3-Flash-GSQ-RCO-3.0bit-Q4Kattn-GGUF). | 114 GB | 96 GB |
| `UD-IQ2_XXS` | The whole model at about 2 bits: [Unsloth's](https://huggingface.co/unsloth/GLM-5.3-Flash-GGUF) quantization, in four files. Its experts are i-quants, which pass through the card faster and through the CPU more slowly than K-quants: it reads a prompt faster than `3.0BIT` and writes more slowly. | 102 GB | 82 GB |
| `REAP50-Q3_K_M` | [REAP50](https://huggingface.co/patrickbdevaney/GLM-5.3-Flash-REAP50-GGUF) by patrickbdevaney: half of each layer's routed experts removed, the rest as 3-bit K-quants. Its card says what was removed and how it was checked. | 79 GB | 60 GB |

The RAM is setup's estimate: the experts the card does not hold, and 10 GB beside them. Without `--model` setup
takes the first size whose estimate the PC meets.

The other two sizes on the machine above, measured like the table at the top, twice each with the engine of
2ba29a383baf. Both files carry their own draft block.

REAP50-Q3_K_M writes a 400-token answer at 68 to 69 tokens/s, 52 to 55 after the prompt of 11,673 tokens and 71
to 75 after the one of 29,069, and reads prompts of 3,059, 11,673 and 29,069 tokens at 1,038 to 1,043, 1,279 to
1,285 and 1,253 to 1,257 tokens/s. The card holds 21.6 GiB of its experts, 1,939 of 6,192. By the README's test it
reads 29,364 tokens at 1,289 to 1,292 tokens/s, with the first token after 22.8 to 22.9 s, and writes a short chat
at 62. Against llama-server of the llama.cpp fork the file was made for, on a prompt of 1,678 tokens, the first 63
tokens were the same; that was an earlier build, and it was not compared again.

UD-IQ2_XXS writes the 400-token answer at 49 tokens/s, 39 after the prompt of 11,673 tokens and 54 to 57 after
the one of 29,069, and reads the three prompts at 805 to 812, 1,020 to 1,030 and 1,014 to 1,021. The card holds
19.6 GiB of its experts, 2,768 to 2,771 of 12,384. By the README's test, in three runs in turn with the build
before, it reads 29,364 tokens at 1,062 to 1,076 tokens/s, with the first token after 27.4 to 27.8 s, and writes a
short chat at 50 to 51.

The build of 2026-10-06 wrote the 400-token answer at 61 tokens/s with REAP50-Q3_K_M and at 44 to 45 with
UD-IQ2_XXS, and read the 29,069-token prompt at 1,207 to 1,209 and 973 to 983. An earlier build read it at 384
tokens/s with REAP50-Q3_K_M and at 347 with UD-IQ2_XXS, and wrote 27 and 33.

## Install

```
./setup.sh --family glm
```

Setup checks the PC and asks three things: the size, the context, and whether to keep several conversations in
RAM. Then it
compiles the engine, downloads the model, checks its SHA-256 and starts it. For 3.0BIT it also downloads the model's
draft block, 2.8 GB, once for every size that needs it. The web app is at
`http://127.0.0.1:8080`. After that, `./setup.sh` starts the model again without asking.

- The compile needs g++ and the CUDA toolkit (12.8 or newer for an RTX 50 card). Setup offers to install what is
  missing. The ready-made `strata-poly` of a release is for Windows only.
- You already have the GGUF: `./setup.sh --family glm --gguf-dir /folder/with/the/file`.
- The other options are the README's: `--context`, `--port`, `--host`, `--api-key`, `--gpu`, `--gpus`,
  `--layer-split`, `--yes`, `--no-start`.
  The ones for the Qwen engine (`--kv`, `--vision`, `--low-ram`, `--parallel`, ...) are named and left out.

On the test machine, with the file already on the disk, this took about five minutes.

Setup writes the run config `strata-glm-3.0bit.json` and `run-glm-3.0bit.sh` into the PolyStrata folder, the engine into
`engine-poly/`, and the tokenizer and the expert profile into the data folder. The profile starts as the counts in
`data/expert-profile-glm.txt`, which come from the test runs here, and the engine updates it after an answer, every
10 seconds at most, and when it stops.

A 64K or 128K context starts and answers, with an expert cache 0.8 or 2.6 GiB smaller. No long prompt has been read
at those sizes.

## Without setup

Build the engine:

```
cmake -S . -B build -G Ninja -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release -DSTRATA_ENABLE_CUDA=ON
cmake --build build --target strata-poly
```

Write the tokenizer out of the GGUF once:

```
python tools/strata_tokenizer.py --gguf /models/GLM-5.3-Flash-GSQ-RCO-3.0bit-q4kattn.gguf --out glm-tokenizer
```

Then write a run config, `glm.json`:

```json
{
  "exe": "build/strata-poly",
  "cwd": "/path/to/PolyStrata",
  "args": ["--model", "/models/GLM-5.3-Flash-GSQ-RCO-3.0bit-q4kattn.gguf",
           "--max-context", "32768",
           "--expert-profile", "glm.profile",
           "--conversation-cache-mib", "8192"],
  "tokenizer": "glm-tokenizer/tokenizer",
  "model_name": "glm-5.3-flash",
  "log": "glm-engine.log"
}
```

and start the server with it:

```
python serve/server.py --engine strata --config glm.json --port 8095
```

The web app is at `http://127.0.0.1:8095`, and the API is the one the README describes. Chat, thinking, tool
calls and streaming were checked through `/v1/chat/completions`. `"enable_thinking": false` in
`chat_template_kwargs` turns thinking off.

## How it uses the card

The model has 45 layers. 42 of them choose 8 of 288 experts for every token, which makes 12,096 experts and most
of the file. The rest of the weights are always in VRAM.

- **The expert cache.** The experts used most often are kept in VRAM: about 2,470 of them, 19.2 GiB, on the 32 GB
  card at a 32K context with the draft block. `--expert-profile FILE` is where the engine keeps count of which ones are used. It reads the
  file at the start to choose, and writes it back when it stops. Without a profile it has no counts yet and keeps
  the same number of experts from every layer, so the second start is the one to measure.
- **The cache follows the answer.** The choice at the start fits the average answer, and some answers badly: of
  the expert uses of a 400-token answer with code in it, 20% found their expert in VRAM. So while an answer is
  written, the engine counts which experts the last few hundred tokens used. The ones they keep choosing are copied
  into VRAM, each over a cached expert they chose less than half as often, up to 16 on their way at a time. For
  that answer the share went to 50% and the speed from 26.4 to 30.7 tokens/s. The cache stays as the last answer
  left it, so the next one starts from there. A prompt does not move it. `--expert-swaps 0` leaves the cache as it
  was loaded, and the same prompt then gets the same answer from the second request on: an expert's result
  differs in its last digits between the CPU and the card, and with swaps an expert can run on one in one run and
  on the other in the next. The first request can still differ, since the cache is sized once more before the
  second. A prompt of 32 tokens or more needs `--cpu-share 0` beside it: the CPU's share of such a call's experts
  follows the calls before.
- **Writing an answer.** An expert that is not in VRAM runs on the CPU while the card runs its share. The CPU reads
  such an expert from memory, and on a PC with more than one NUMA node that memory is on the node whose CPU first
  read the file. The engine looks where the experts' pages are and keeps its CPU threads on that node: from the
  other node the same work took 30% longer on the machine above. `--any-node` turns that off.
- **Guessing ahead.** While an answer is written, the engine guesses the next few tokens and puts the token it
  knows and up to three guesses through the model together. A guess counts only when it is the token the model
  picks itself, so the answer is the same with guessing and without. The guesses come from two places. An answer
  often repeats text the conversation already has, code it was asked to change or a passage it quotes: when the
  last few tokens match an earlier place, what followed there is the guess. And the model has a block made for
  guessing, one more layer that takes a token and what the model made of the token before it, and says what is
  likely to come next. The block is the model's, one for every size. Unsloth's files carry it. The 3.0BIT file
  leaves it out, so setup downloads the block alone for it, 2.8 GB out of Unsloth's files, and gives it to the
  engine as `--draft-model`. REAP50 has its own, pruned like its layers. A right guess saves less than a whole
  token, because its experts outside VRAM still run on the CPU: four tokens together take about three times as
  long as one. Measured on the 32 GB card with an earlier build, 600-token answers, twice each, in tokens/s:

  | Answer | 3.0BIT without guesses | with | REAP50-Q3_K_M without | with |
  | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
  | plain text | 40 | 46 to 47 | 41 | 44 to 46 |
  | quotes from a document | 42 | 49 to 51 | 41 | 48 to 51 |
  | a 670-token file given back with one name changed | 42 | 51 to 52 | 42 | 50 to 59 |

  Of 3.0BIT's guesses 87 to 98% were right. UD-IQ2_XXS gains little: its experts are i-quants, which the CPU
  computes more slowly, so a guessed token costs it nearly what a token costs. Three 200-token answers wrote 30.7,
  33.7 and 31.4 tokens/s with its block and 31.3, 30.9 and 31.4 without. `--no-draft` and `--lookup 0` turn the two
  sources off.
- **Reading a prompt.** The prompt goes in up to 1,024 tokens at a time. For each layer the experts outside the
  cache are copied through a buffer in VRAM and run there. A larger batch reads faster and leaves less VRAM for the
  expert cache, so answers get slower: with an earlier build, which read the 11,673-token prompt at 334 tokens/s
  and wrote about 27, 4,096 at a time read it at 396 and wrote 18.5.

## Several conversations at once

The engine holds one conversation. A coding agent rarely sends just one: subagents and short side requests come in
between the turns of the main one. Each switch used to make the engine read the whole prompt again.

`--conversation-cache-mib N` gives the engine N MiB of RAM to park conversations in. When another conversation
needs the engine, the current one is copied to RAM. When it comes back it is copied in again and goes on where it
stopped. Two conversations that start with the same text, a shared system prompt for one, share what is saved for
that part.

Measured through the server with 8,192 MiB: a main conversation of 3,884 prompt tokens, a subagent of 3,323 and a
short request took turns. Every later turn of the first two had 97.8 to 99.2% of its prompt cached and took 1.3 to
3.9 s. The first turns, which read the prompt, took 13 s.

What it costs: a checkpoint is 153 MB of RAM, and a parked conversation takes 28 KB for each token. At the end of
that run two parked conversations held 1.1 GB.

The API reports what was not read again as `usage.prompt_tokens_details.cached_tokens` (Responses:
`input_tokens_details.cached_tokens`, Messages: `cache_read_input_tokens`), and the web app's Monitor has a
Conversation cache card.

It is off by default, as it is in `strata`. Without it the engine still keeps the last conversation: a next turn
goes on from where it stopped, and an edit goes back to the nearest checkpoint.

## Options

| Option | Default | What it does |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| `--model FILE.gguf` | | the model |
| `--serve` | | take requests from the server (the server passes this) |
| `--max-context N` | 8192 | tokens a conversation can hold |
| `--threads N` | all usable | CPU threads for the experts that are not in VRAM |
| `--vram-reserve-mib N` | a thirty-second of the card, 256 to 768, or a sixteenth, 512 to 1024, kept whole, when the PC's free RAM is within 4 GiB of the model's files; else half of it after the first request | VRAM left free beside the expert cache |
| `--layer-split S` | the first card alone | the model's layers across the cards the engine sees: `auto` (by each card's free VRAM) or the first layer of each later card, as `20` or `20,30` ([MULTI_GPU.md](MULTI_GPU.md#the-strata-poly-models)) |
| `--expert-profile FILE` | | expert use counts, read at the start and written back after an answer, every 10 seconds at most, and at the end |
| `--no-gpu-experts` | | keep every routed expert on the CPU |
| `--any-node` | | on a PC with several NUMA nodes, let the CPU threads run on any of them |
| `--no-graphs` | | launch a token's kernels one by one, not as the CUDA graph a layer that is recorded at the first token |
| `--expert-swaps N` | 64 | while an answer is written, experts on their way into VRAM at a time, each in place of a cached one the answer uses less; 0 = the cache stays as it was loaded |
| `--window N` | 4; 8 for the other models | the most tokens that go through the model together while an answer is written: the known one and guesses after it: the draft block's three at most, and more than three only from a repeat of eight tokens or more; 1 = no guesses |
| `--lookup N` | 3 | guess what followed an earlier repeat of the last N or more tokens; 0 = never |
| `--no-draft` | | do not run the block that guesses ahead |
| `--draft-model FILE.gguf` | | a file of the same model to take that block from, when `--model` has none; setup passes the one it downloaded |
| `--draft-vocab N` | 131072 for Qwen3.6, Ornith and Tiel Coder, all of it for the others | the block guesses among the vocabulary's first N tokens; 0 = all |
| `--kv-whole` | on an AMD card | give the attention caches VRAM for the whole context at load, not as the conversation grows; setup writes it for an AMD card, where ROCm keeps the VRAM the expert cache gives back ([AMD_HIP.md](AMD_HIP.md#strata-poly-on-amd-beta)) |
| `--kv fmt` | fp16 | the K and V attention caches' format: `fp16` (default, unchanged) or `q8_0` (ggml Q8_0 blocks: one fp16 scale a 32-value block of int8 codes): half the KV VRAM, the same answers on the tested models ([Q8_KV.md](Q8_KV.md)) |
| `--prompt-batch N` | 1024 | prompt tokens read at a time, 1 to 4096; about 1.4 MiB of VRAM each |
| `--stage-mib N` | 2048 | VRAM the experts outside the cache pass through while a prompt is read |
| `--stage-ahead N` | 1024 | from N tokens, a batch copies those experts before the layer has chosen, as does every batch of a prompt of N tokens or more that is read several at a time; 0 = never |
| `--cpu-share F` | as the times say | in a batch of fewer tokens than `--stage-ahead`, the share of the experts outside VRAM that the CPU computes while the others are copied: the ones the fewest tokens chose; 0 = none |
| `--prompt-cache N` | 6 | checkpoints kept of a conversation; 0 = only a next turn is not read again |
| `--prompt-cache-every N` | 2048 | a checkpoint every N prompt tokens, besides the one at a prompt's end; 0 = none |
| `--conversation-cache-mib N` | 0 | RAM for parked conversations; 0 = off |
| `--conversation-cache-slots N` | 4 | parked conversations at most |
| `--conversation-cache-min-tokens N` | 1024 | a shorter conversation is not parked |
| `--conversation-cache-min-free-mib N` | 2560 | park only while this much physical RAM stays free |
| `--eos-ids ID,ID,...` | from the GGUF | tokens that end a reply |
| `--tail-skip N` | 0; setup gives Qwen3.6 6 | an expert outside VRAM that every token of a window ranks at place N+1 or later among its experts is left out: faster, and the answers move a little |
| `STRATA_FREEZE_EXPERTS` | not set | hold the split of the routed experts between the card and the CPU still for the engine's whole run, so a greedy answer is a function of the request alone: same request, same answer, bit for bit. With guesses on (`--window` above 1 and a block or `--lookup`) the block's guesses and their windows still take their sizes from the machine's pace, which the freeze does not hold: on the cards measured so far it repeated with them on (RX 9060 XT, several thread counts and loads) and on one RTX 4070 laptop it did not — add `--window 1` for a repeat wherever the guesses are on. Measured cost: nothing measurable on the default path, 4-7% at a short prompt with the freeze set |

`strata-poly` with no arguments prints the list, with the options for running it without the server.

`--kv q8_0` is new: it stores the attention K and V caches as ggml Q8_0 blocks and is opt-in for the
`qwen35moe` models. [Q8_KV.md](Q8_KV.md) has the format, the measured VRAM saving (46.88% at 131072), the
fp16↔q8_0 parity on Tiel, Ornith and Occamy, and what was left alone (the `strata` engine, `glm5next`, Gemma 4,
the default path).

## What is missing

- Other files of the model. The engine reads the layouts of four converters (neurall's llama.cpp fork,
  patrickbdevaney's, antirez's and Unsloth's), a model in several files, and the weight types llama.cpp's kernels
  have. Only the three sizes above have been run: setup offers no other.
- A ready-made `strata-poly` off Windows, or for a driver older than CUDA 13's: setup compiles it there.
- GLM on Windows, by this project: no PC with the RAM for it was at hand, and setup asks before it installs it
  there. A user ran 3.0BIT on Windows 10 with an RTX 5090 D v2 (24 GB) and 128 GB of RAM
  ([issue 10](https://github.com/VecSzn/PolyStrata/issues/10)): 1,483 experts in VRAM, 26 to 30 tokens/s after
  a short prompt, 10K tokens read at 375 to 395. The context set to 1,048,576 in the run config loaded and
  answered, and one request of 557K tokens was read at 286 tokens/s with the three facts planted in it found.
  With 0.1.44.1-poly.5 a request of 1,002,452 tokens was read in 45.7 minutes (66.3 with poly.2), the three
  facts found, and from the second request after it the engine wrote at 25 to 30 tokens/s again.
- Intel cards, and AMD cards on Windows. On Linux setup compiles the engine for an AMD card
  ([AMD_HIP.md](AMD_HIP.md#strata-poly-on-amd-beta)), a beta tested with Qwen3.6 and Ornith on an RX 7600; GLM
  itself has not been tried on one, and setup says so. Two NVIDIA cards can share the model
  ([MULTI_GPU.md](MULTI_GPU.md#the-strata-poly-models)): REAP50 has run on two 10 GB cards under Linux.
- Pictures. The model's image input is not read.
- Several requests decoded together (`"parallel"`). Requests wait their turn.
- `--prompt-cache-root` and `--turn-token`. Checkpoints are taken by position.


# DeepSeek V4 Flash (`strata-poly`)

`strata-poly`, the engine that runs GLM 5.3 Flash, also runs DeepSeek V4 Flash: llama.cpp's architecture
`deepseek4`, read from Unsloth's GGUF. The server, the web app and the API are the ones the README describes.

It is early. This project has run it on one machine, with Linux and one NVIDIA card; a user runs it on Windows
with a 24 GB card and 128 GB of RAM ([issue 21](https://github.com/VecSzn/PolyStrata/issues/21)).

## What it was measured on

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Graphics card | RTX 5090, 32 GB |
| CPU | Xeon Platinum 8470Q, 25 vCPU of it (a cloud machine) |
| RAM | 120 GiB |
| Built with | CUDA 12.8 |
| Model | `DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731-UD-IQ2_XXS`, three files, 90.9 GB, from [unsloth/DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731-GGUF](https://huggingface.co/unsloth/DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731-GGUF) |
| Context | 32K |

| | `strata-poly` | llama.cpp, 10 layers' experts on the card | llama.cpp, every expert on the CPU |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Writes answers | 48 to 53 tokens/s | 25 to 28 tokens/s | 20 to 21 tokens/s |
| Reads a prompt of 3,384 tokens | 1,003 tokens/s | 96 tokens/s | 56 tokens/s |
| Reads a prompt of 10,398 tokens | 1,147 tokens/s | | |
| Starts | 6 to 7 s with the files in the page cache | | |

All of it ran on the machine above, with 24 CPU threads. The engine's numbers are with the expert profile it
writes as it runs; the first answers after a start without one came at 42 to 43 tokens/s. For llama.cpp a small
program drives its library and its own `deepseek4` graph and writes greedy tokens, with the routed experts of the
first layers in RAM, as `--n-cpu-moe` keeps them. The experts of ten layers are what its 32 GB card had room for
beside the rest of the model at this context. That column is the faster of two runs; the other wrote 24 to 26
tokens/s and read the prompt at 72.

The README's row is PolyStrata's own benchmark through the server, the test every model of this engine gets
([BACKENDS.md](BACKENDS.md#a-prompt-of-30000-tokens)), as setup installs the model: a short chat written at 56
tokens/s, and a prompt of 29,750 tokens read whole at 1,038 to 1,040, with the first token after 29 s and the
answer after it written at 39 to 44: the second and third of three runs, as for the other models. The first, with
no expert profile yet, wrote the short chat at 56.5.

With greedy sampling the engine and llama.cpp write the same tokens until two candidates are nearly tied: 8 of 8,
64 of 64 and 10 tokens on three short prompts, 15 of 32 on the prompt of 3,384 tokens and all 32 on the one of
10,398. Only the long prompts reach far enough back to use the compressed rows, the indexer's pick and the rows of
128 positions, so those agree as well.

## What you need

- Linux and an NVIDIA card, RTX 20 or newer. It has run on 32 GB of VRAM only. About 11.5 GB go to the weights,
  the caches and the buffers before any expert, so setup stops and asks below 13.5 GB.
- RAM for the experts the card does not hold. The files are mapped and those experts are read from them while the
  model answers, so they should stay in the OS file cache: about 62 GB of the 84 GB on a 32 GB card. Setup's
  estimate for that card is 72 GB of RAM. It has run with 120 GB only.
- 91 GB of disk for the three files.

## Install

```
./setup.sh --family deepseek
```

Setup checks the PC and asks two things: the context, and whether to keep several conversations in RAM. There is
one size so far, so it does not ask for one. Then it compiles the engine, downloads the three files, checks their
SHA-256 and starts the model. The web app is at `http://127.0.0.1:8080`, and `./setup.sh` starts the model again
without asking.

- You already have the files: `./setup.sh --family deepseek --gguf-dir /folder/with/the/files`, the three files
  under their names from Unsloth's repository.
- The other options are GLM's ([GLM.md](GLM.md#install)).

On the test machine, with the files already on the disk, this took about four minutes: two for the compile, one and a
half for the files' SHA-256.

Setup writes the run config `strata-dsv4-ud-iq2_xxs.json` and `run-dsv4-ud-iq2_xxs.sh` into the PolyStrata folder.
No expert profile is shipped: the engine starts one at the first start and keeps it up to date.

## Long contexts

Setup offers contexts up to 128K; `--context` takes up to 1,048,576 (`./setup.sh --family deepseek --context 524288`),
as does `--max-context` in the run config. A context token takes 6,880 bytes of VRAM, so on the 32 GB card even
512K leaves the expert cache most of its room: 3.4 GB less than at 32K.

Two prompts made of this repository's documents, with three facts planted at 15%, 50% and 85% of them and three
questions after them, through the server with thinking off:

| Context | Prompt | Read and answered in | Facts found |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 128K | 116,473 tokens | 174 s | 3 of 3 |
| 512K | 487,529 tokens | 1,923 s | 3 of 3 |

A prompt is read more slowly as it grows. The indexer scores every compressed row of its layers for every token, so
its work grows with the square of the prompt, and the layers of 128 positions read more rows too. Near the end of
the 487,529-token prompt the engine read about 150 tokens per second.

## How it differs from the other models

The engine and its options are GLM's ([GLM.md](GLM.md#how-it-uses-the-card)). What is DeepSeek V4's own:

- **The attention keeps little per token.** A token reads the last 128 positions in full, and the rest of the
  conversation as compressed rows: in 21 of the 43 layers one row for every 4 positions, of which an indexer picks
  the 512 the token reads, and in 20 others one row for every 128 positions, all of which it reads. The first two
  layers read the last 128 positions only. All 64 heads share one key row, which is also the value. A context token
  takes 6,880 bytes of VRAM, so a 128K context takes 0.7 GB more than 32K.
- **The experts.** 43 layers, each with 256 routed experts of which a token uses 6, and one shared expert.
  The first three layers pick a token's experts by the token, from a table in the file. On the 32 GB card about
  2,840 of the 11,008 experts stay in VRAM at a 32K context.
- **Four residual streams**, mixed by hyper-connections, as in GLM 5.3 Flash.
- **Two layers converted at load.** In Unsloth's file the down experts of layers 26 and 42 are MXFP4, which no
  kernel here runs on the card. Every MXFP4 value in them is a small integer times a power of two that Q5_0 holds
  exactly, so the engine converts them once at load into 1.38 GiB of RAM each and runs them like the other layers.
  Before the conversion those two layers took more of a long prompt's time than the other 41 together.
- **Reading a prompt** goes 1,024 tokens at a time, as for GLM. The attention of a batch takes 8 heads of a token
  at a time, so that the key rows they share are read once for all 8.
- **Tool calls** come in DeepSeek's own format, DSML, which the server turns into OpenAI's and Anthropic's tool
  calls. Thinking is on unless a request turns it off; `"high"` adds the template's own instruction to think
  thoroughly.

## What is missing

- Other sizes in setup: of those in Unsloth's repository it offers only UD-IQ2_XXS. A user with 128 GB of RAM ran
  two more ([issue 21](https://github.com/VecSzn/PolyStrata/issues/21)).
  UD-IQ3_XXS (104 GB) wrote at 27 to 31 tokens/s and read 512K tokens 4.6% slower than UD-IQ2_XXS.
  UD-IQ4_XS (137 GB) is larger than that RAM: the rest is read from the disk as it is needed, and it wrote at 2 to
  3.4 tokens/s with 0.1.44.1-poly.6. llama.cpp b11706 with the experts on the CPU wrote at 8.6 with the same
  file, and strata-poly at 1.4 to 2.2 against llama.cpp's 9.5 to 10.6 with UD-Q4_K_XL (155 GB), the same
  prompt on the same PC. The draft model (dspark) slowed llama.cpp down there, to 2.0 to 4.4 tokens/s.
- A way of running a file larger than the RAM that comes near llama.cpp's speed there (above).
- Guessing ahead with a draft model. The repository has one for this model (`dspark`, 11 GB), which the engine does
  not run yet. Guesses still come from text the conversation repeats.
- Windows, by this project: setup asks before it installs it there. A user ran UD-IQ2_XXS on Windows 10 with an
  RTX 5090 D v2 (24 GB) and 128 GB of RAM on the release's ready-made engine ([issue 21](https://github.com/VecSzn/PolyStrata/issues/21)): 29 to 36
  tokens/s after prompts of up to 10K tokens, 10K tokens read at 688 to 805. With the context set to 262,144,
  524,288 and 1,048,576 in the run config, one request each of 251,866, 503,920 and 1,008,128 tokens was read at
  393, 247 and 138 tokens/s, and the three facts planted in each were found.
- AMD cards: the engine compiles for them, the model has not been tried on one.
- Pictures. The model reads text only.
- A faster indexer for long prompts: it runs on the card's ordinary cores, not its tensor cores.


# LFM2.5-24B-A2B (`strata-poly`)

`strata-poly`, the engine of GLM 5.3 Flash and the smaller models, also runs Liquid AI's LFM2.5-24B-A2B: llama.cpp's
architecture `lfm2moe`, read from the GGUF files of Unsloth and Liquid AI. The server, the web app and the API are the
ones the README describes.

It is new here. This project has run it on two machines: a Windows laptop whose card has 8 GB, and a Linux
workstation with a 32 GB card.

Its license is Liquid AI's LFM Open License v1.0, not an open-source one: commercial use is licensed only to
companies under 10 million US dollars of yearly revenue
([the license](https://huggingface.co/LiquidAI/LFM2.5-24B-A2B/blob/main/LICENSE)).

## The sizes

| Size | From | Download | Experts | RAM beside an 8 GB card (setup's estimate) |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| **UD-Q4_K_XL** | [Unsloth](https://huggingface.co/unsloth/LFM2.5-24B-A2B-GGUF): 4-bit experts, 5- and 6-bit where they write | 14.9 GB | 14.0 GB | about 20 GB |
| **UD-Q3_K_XL** | Unsloth: 3-bit i-quant experts, 4-bit where they write | 10.9 GB | 10.2 GB | about 16 GB |
| **QAD-Q4_0** | [Liquid AI](https://huggingface.co/LiquidAI/LFM2.5-24B-A2B-GGUF): Q4_0, trained for it (quantization-aware distillation) | 13.6 GB | 12.9 GB | about 18 GB |
| **UD-Q6_K_XL** | Unsloth: 6-bit experts, 8-bit where they write | 21.9 GB | 20.9 GB | about 27 GB |
| **Q8_0** | Unsloth: 8-bit throughout | 25.5 GB | 24.4 GB | about 31 GB |

Setup takes UD-Q4_K_XL, or UD-Q3_K_XL when the PC has too little RAM for it; the others are chosen by name
(`--model QAD-Q4_0`). Every file is pinned to its repository's commit of 2026-10-15 and checked against its SHA-256.

## How fast

The README's numbers are PolyStrata's own benchmark through the server, the test every model of this engine gets
([BACKENDS.md](BACKENDS.md#a-prompt-of-30000-tokens)): `polystrata.py benchmark --prompt-tokens 24000`, a short chat
and a prompt built from this repository's documents, 256 tokens written after each, three runs a size and the last
two below. The engine is 4a644a8d's, with the run configs setup writes for each machine: on both, a prompt is read
2,048 tokens at a time.

### On a laptop with 8 GB of VRAM

An RTX 4070 Laptop with 8 GB, an i7-14700HX, 32 GB of RAM, Windows 10, CUDA 13.0. A context of 32,768 and 13 threads
for the experts the card does not hold. The prompt came to 29,804 tokens.

| Size | First token, s | Reads, tokens/s | Writes a short chat | Writes after the prompt | Experts from VRAM | Machine's RAM, GiB |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| UD-Q4_K_XL | 8.3 to 8.4 | 3,575 to 3,601 | 67 to 68 | 69 | 52% to 53% | 22.5 to 22.6 |
| UD-Q3_K_XL | 7.9 to 8.0 | 3,758 to 3,811 | 80 to 81 | 70 to 76 | 67% | 18.7 to 18.8 |
| QAD-Q4_0 | 8.1 | 3,685 to 3,694 | 72 to 73 | 77 to 79 | 59% | 21.1 to 21.2 |
| UD-Q6_K_XL | 9.6 to 9.9 | 3,035 to 3,115 | 41 to 44 | 53 to 54 | 36% | 29.1 |
| Q8_0 | 10.3 to 12.4 | 2,406 to 2,923 | 19 to 34 | 44 to 47 | 30% to 31% | 31.2 |

Q8_0 fills the machine's RAM, and part of its file is read from the disk again; that is what makes it the slowest
here, and the most uneven. Four passes before this one, with earlier engines, read the prompt at 1,290 to 3,778
tokens/s, the last of them, d74c5e25's with the same batch, at 2,954 to 3,778. A README cell has the highest of the
five passes: the short chat came a little faster in an earlier one with most sizes.

### On a workstation

An RTX 5090 (32 GB), 25 vCPU of a Xeon Platinum 8470Q, 120 GB of RAM, Linux, CUDA 12.8. The card holds every expert
of every size. The prompt came to 29,816 tokens.

| Size | First token, s | Reads, tokens/s | Writes a short chat | Writes after the prompt | Experts from VRAM |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| UD-Q4_K_XL | 1.3 to 1.4 | 23,387 to 23,462 | 480 to 485 | 639 to 640 | 100% |
| UD-Q3_K_XL | 1.3 to 1.4 | 22,923 to 23,396 | 455 to 472 | 663 to 675 | 100% |
| QAD-Q4_0 | 1.3 | 23,878 to 23,899 | 495 to 497 | 419 to 420 | 100% |
| UD-Q6_K_XL | 1.5 | 21,252 to 21,270 | 423 to 426 | 366 to 376 | 100% |
| Q8_0 | 1.4 | 23,272 to 23,349 | 376 | 341 to 343 | 100% |

The answer after the prompt goes faster than the short chat where it repeats the prompt's text and the engine's
guesses from it are right. One token at a time (`--window 1`), UD-Q4_K_XL writes at 452 tokens/s after a short
prompt and at 387 after 29,816 tokens. Three passes before this one, with earlier engines, read the prompt at 10,007
to 21,666 tokens/s; a README cell has the highest of the four.

### Against llama.cpp

On the laptop, UD-Q4_K_XL through each server with the same prompts
([the runs](../bench/results/2026-10-15-laptop-lfm25-llamacpp-b11540/README.md)):

| | PolyStrata | llama.cpp b11540, `--n-cpu-moe 25` | llama.cpp b11540, `--fit on` |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Reads about 4,000 tokens | 2,193 to 2,215 tokens/s | 498 to 505 | 483 to 485 |
| Writes after them | 89 to 94 tokens/s | 57.7 to 58.0 | 55.1 to 55.3 |
| Reads about 32,000 tokens | 3,543 to 3,545 tokens/s | 528 to 537 | 505 to 516 |
| Writes after them | 71 to 73 tokens/s | 52.8 to 53.0 | 50.9 to 51.0 |

`--n-cpu-moe 25` was the fastest of the settings tried for llama.cpp there. On the workstation, `llama-bench` of the
llama.cpp that [Strata](https://github.com/Niko1221/Strata) v0.1.39 vendors, every layer on the card and flash
attention on:

| | PolyStrata | llama.cpp, batch of 512 (its default) | llama.cpp, batch of 2,048 |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Reads 29,816 tokens | 23,400 tokens/s (18,700 with a batch of 1,024) | 13,311 | 23,614 |
| Writes, one token at a time | 452 tokens/s | 455 | |
| Writes after 29,816 tokens, one at a time | 387 tokens/s | 369 | |

With a batch of 2,048 llama.cpp reads the prompt as fast as PolyStrata, 1% faster, and writes as fast one token
at a time; after the long prompt PolyStrata writes 5% faster. Its guesses add to that where the answer repeats the
prompt.

## Long contexts

LFM2.5 was trained for 131,072 tokens, and setup offers a context up to that. The test is the one DEEPSEEK.md has:
a document of this repository's docs with three facts planted at 15%, 50% and 85% of it, then three questions.
UD-Q4_K_XL, a context of 131,072 and prompts read 2,048 tokens at a time:

- On the workstation, through the server, the 123,609-token request was read and answered in 8.7 s. The answer had
  the second and the third fact and made the first one up.
- Given the prompt as llama.cpp tokenizes it, 123,602 tokens with thinking off, the engine wrote all three facts
  greedily. llama.cpp's own CUDA build (llama-server of 86ebfef, flash attention on, the same file) wrote the second
  and the third and made the first one up. The fact furthest back is where the model is least sure.
- On the laptop the same tokens were read in 44.7 and 45.4 s, 2,700 tokens/s, and the answer written at 35 tokens/s;
  its first 80 tokens had the first two facts.

## How it was checked

- The tokenizer gives the same ids as llama.cpp's `llama-tokenize` on 174,442 tokens: three READMEs (English,
  Chinese, Japanese), BACKENDS.md, a Python file and a CUDA file.
- With greedy sampling the engine and llama.cpp on the CPU write the same tokens until two candidates are close: 1,
  39 and 10 of 64 tokens on three short prompts, 20 on a prompt of 17,575 tokens. llama.cpp's CUDA build parts
  from its CPU build at 5, 40 and 23 tokens on the same three. While they agree on the long prompt, the
  probabilities are 0.087 apart at most.
- A window of guesses writes the same tokens as one token at a time, with the experts on the CPU: 300 tokens after
  a prompt of 26 tokens and after one of 4,259.
- `poly_attend_test`, `poly_norm_q8_test`, `poly_shortconv_test` and `poly_route_test` check the kernels written for
  it against the ones they stand for; `serve/test_lfm2.py` the tool calls and the chat template.

## How the sizes compare

Q8_0 wrote 256 greedy tokens on each of five prompts (code, a summary of 4,260 tokens of BACKENDS.md, arithmetic,
a story, a question in Chinese); each other size then followed those tokens, and the share of steps where its most
likely token is Q8_0's was counted. Q8_0 stands in for the model as trained here.

| Size | Same most likely token as Q8_0 | Mean probability of Q8_0's token |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| UD-Q6_K_XL | 96.8% (1,239 of 1,280) | 0.782 |
| UD-Q4_K_XL | 95.5% (1,222 of 1,280) | 0.781 |
| QAD-Q4_0 | 92.7% (1,186 of 1,280) | 0.762 |
| UD-Q3_K_XL | 91.6% (1,173 of 1,280) | 0.766 |

## What was tuned for it

The decode numbers are UD-Q4_K_XL on the RTX 5090 with the engine's guesses, after a short prompt and after one of
4,300 tokens, tokens/s:

| Commit | Change | Effect |
| --- | --- | --- |
| d4d5049c | the model runs | 386 and 347 |
| 6af34f82 | a layer's residual sum, RMS norm and Q8_1 quantization in one launch | 401 and 359 |
| 962da9b9 | the router's kernel with as few slots a lane as 64 experts need | 405 and 368 |
| 72a738f6, 165bb4da | with every expert on the card, the card picks a few tokens' experts itself, and a token's 40 layers run as one recorded launch | 454 and 408 |
| 4f5947f5 | the attention of a few tokens in blocks of 64 rows, a launch block each | one token at a time after 4,300 tokens 381 to 441, after 30,000 196 to 350; the laptop after 30,000 49 to 56 |
| d74c5e25 | a prompt batch's attention in registers, the way FlashAttention-2 goes | a prompt of 29,816 tokens read in 1,852 ms instead of 2,787 on the 5090, in 8.9 s instead of 16.8 on the laptop |
| 0ec4802d | setup's config reads 2,048 tokens at a time on a card that holds every expert | 1,415 ms instead of 1,852 |
| 36fdc62e | and on any card from 8 GB | the laptop 7.8 to 8.0 s instead of 8.9 to 9.1 |
| 3d51cba9 | the blocks of rows added up 16 warps at a time | one token at a time after 30,000 tokens 348 to 388 |
| cd87aecd | the next block of keys copied in while attn_flash64 works on one | 1,361 to 1,371 ms instead of 1,398 to 1,400 |
| 4a644a8d | a prompt batch's cached experts in three widths of tiles instead of nine | 1,280 ms instead of 1,367 to 1,376 |

## What you need

- An NVIDIA card, RTX 20 or newer, with 8 GB of VRAM or more. About 4 GB go to the weights outside the experts, the
  caches and the buffers at a 32K context; the rest holds experts. A 24 GB card holds the three smaller sizes
  whole, by setup's estimate.
- RAM for the experts the card does not hold. They are read from the mapped file while the model answers, so they
  should stay in the OS file cache: setup's estimate is in the table above. All five sizes have run on the laptop
  with 32 GB, but Q8_0 fills it and is slow there.
- Linux or Windows 10 / 11. AMD cards: the engine compiles for them on Linux, and this model has not been tried on
  one; setup says so.

## Install

```
./setup.sh --family lfm25
```

On Windows, `START-HERE.bat` and pick it from the list. Setup asks for the size, the context and whether to keep
several conversations in RAM, downloads the file, checks its SHA-256, writes the tokenizer and the run config
`strata-lfm25-<size>.json`, and starts the model. `--model UD-Q3_K_XL` (or another size) skips the question, and
`--gguf-dir <folder>` uses a file you already have, under its name from the repository.

## How it differs from the other models

- **Most layers have no attention.** 30 of its 40 layers mix the tokens with a short convolution over the last
  three: each projects a token to three vectors B, C and x, convolves B times x, and scales the result by C. Its
  state is two vectors a layer. The other 10 layers attend over the whole conversation, with 8 key heads of 64
  values, so a context token takes 20,480 bytes of VRAM: a 128K context takes 2.0 GB more than 32K.
- **The experts.** 38 layers have 64 routed experts each, of which a token uses 4, with sigmoid gates, a bias that
  steers the pick and weights that sum to one. There is no shared expert. The first two layers have a plain FFN.
  The output matrix is the token table.
